## ![](_page_0_Picture_2.jpeg)

# [Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Main_Page)

Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva (Brazilian Portuguese: [\[lu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Portuguese)ˈi[z](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Portuguese) iˈ[nasju](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Portuguese) ˈ[lul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Portuguese)[ɐ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Portuguese) d[ɐ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Portuguese) ˈ[siwv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Portuguese)ɐ] [ⓘ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Pt-Luiz_Inacio_Lula_da_Silva.ogg); born Luiz Inácio da Silva; 27 October 1945),[\[1\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-Britannica-1) also known as Lula da Silva or simply Lula, is a Brazilian politician who is the 39th and incumbent [President of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Brazil) since 2023. A member of the [Workers'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Workers%27_Party_(Brazil)) [Party,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Workers%27_Party_(Brazil)) Lula was also the 35th president from 2003 to 2010. He also holds the presidency of the [G20](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G20) since 2023.

Lula quit school after second grade to work, and did not learn to read until he was ten years old. As a teenager, he worked as a metalworker and became a [trade unionist.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_union) Between 1978 and 1980 he led workers' strikes during Brazil's [military dictatorship,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_dictatorship_in_Brazil) and in 1980 he helped start the Workers' Party during Brazil's [political opening.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazil%27s_political_opening) Lula was one of the leaders of the 1984 [Diretas Já](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diretas_J%C3%A1) movement, which demanded direct elections. In [1986,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986_Brazilian_legislative_election) he was elected a federal deputy in the state of [São Paulo.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Paulo_(state)) He ran for president in [1989,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989_Brazilian_presidential_election) but lost in the second round. He went on to also lose two other presidential elections, both in [1994](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_Brazilian_general_election), and then in [1998.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_Brazilian_general_election) He finally became president in [2002,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_Brazilian_presidential_election) in a runoff. In [2006,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Brazilian_presidential_election) he was re-elected in the second round.

Described as left-wing, his first presidency coincided with South America's first [pink tide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pink_tide) and was marked by the consolidation of social welfare programs such as [Bolsa Família](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolsa_Fam%C3%ADlia) and [Fome Zero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fome_Zero). During his two terms in office, he undertook reforms which eventually led to growth in GDP, reduction in public debt and inflation, and helping 20 million Brazilians escape poverty. He also played a role in foreign policy, both on a regional level and as part of global trade and environment negotiations[.\[2\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-2) During his first two terms, Lula was considered one of the most popular politicians in Brazil's history while president, and one of the most popular in the world.[\[3\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto6-3)[\[4\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-4)[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-5)[\[6\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-6) His first term was marked by notable scandals, including the [Mensalão vote-buying scandal.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mensal%C3%A3o_scandal) After the [2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Brazilian_general_election) [Brazilian general election,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Brazilian_general_election) he was succeeded by his former chief of staff, [Dilma Rousse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dilma_Rousseff)ff, and remained active in politics and gave lectures.

In July 2017, Lula was convicted on charges of [money laundering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Money_laundering) and corruption in a trial, after which he spent a total of 580 days in prison. He attempted to run in the [2018 Brazilian presidential](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Brazilian_presidential_election)

## 1 January 2003 – 31 December 2010

[election,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Brazilian_presidential_election) but was disqualified under Brazil's [Ficha Limpa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ficha_Limpa) law; he was released the following year in 2019, and his conviction was nullified in 2021 by the Supreme Court. The same ruling, which also found serious biases in the case against him, also annulled all other pending cases against him. Now legally allowed to make another run for president, he did so in the [2022 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Brazilian_general_election) and ultimately defeated the incumbent [Jair Bolsonaro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jair_Bolsonaro) in a runoff. Sworn in on 1 January 2023 at the age of 77, he is the oldest Brazilian president at time of inauguration, as well as the first-ever candidate to have defeated an incumbent president and be elected to a third term.

# Early life

Luiz Inácio da Silva was born on 27 October 1945 (registered with a date of birth of 6 October 1945) in [Caetés](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caet%C3%A9s,_Pernambuco) (then a district of [Garanhuns\)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garanhuns), 250 km (150 miles) from [Recife,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recife) capital of [Pernambuco,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pernambuco) a state in the [Northeast of Brazil.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_of_Brazil) He was the seventh of eight children of Aristides Inácio da Silva and Eurídice Ferreira de Melo, farmers who experienced the famine in one of the poorest parts of the [agreste.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agreste)[\[7\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-:1-7) He was raised [Roman Catholic.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic)[\[8\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-8) Lula's mother was of [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Brazilian) and partial [Italian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Brazilian) descent.[\[9\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-9) Two weeks after Lula's birth, his father moved to [Santos, São Paulo,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santos,_S%C3%A3o_Paulo) with--though his wife was not aware of it--Valdomira Ferreira de Góis, a younger cousin of his mother.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-10)

In December 1952, when Lula was seven years old, his mother moved the family to [São Paulo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Paulo_(state)) to rejoin her husband. After a journey of 13 days in a pau-de-arara (open [truck bed\)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pickup_truck#Pickup_bed_styles), they arrived in [Guarujá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guaruj%C3%A1) and discovered that Aristides had formed a second family with Valdomira, with whom he had 10 more children.[\[11\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-11) Aristides's two families lived in the same house for some time, but they did not get along very well, and four years later, his mother moved with him and his siblings to a small room behind a bar in São Paulo. After that, Lula rarely saw his father, who died illiterate and an alcoholic in 1978.[\[12\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-12)[\[13\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-story-13) In 1982, he added the nickname Lula to his legal name[.\[14\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-14)

# Personal life

Lula has been married three times, and has a daughter from a fourth relationship. In 1969, he married Maria de Lourdes Ribeiro.[\[15\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-cunning-15) She died of hepatitis in 1971 while pregnant with a child, who also died[.\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-Hoje_em_dia-16)

In March 1974, Lula had an illegitimate daughter, Lurian, with his then-girlfriend, Miriam

Cordeiro.[\[15\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-cunning-15) The two never married. Lula only began participating in his daughter's life when she was already a young adult[.\[17\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-17)

Two months later, in May 1974, Lula married [Marisa Letícia Rocco Casa,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marisa_Let%C3%ADcia_Lula_da_Silva) a 24-year-old widow whom he had met the prior year. He had three sons with her, and adopted her son from her first marriage.[\[15\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-cunning-15) The two remained married for 43 years, until her death on 2 February 2017 after a stroke.[\[18\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-:4-18)

Still in 2017, he met and started a relationship with [Rosângela da Silva,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ros%C3%A2ngela_Lula_da_Silva) known as Janja. The relationship only became public in 2019 while he was serving time in jail in [Curitiba,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curitiba) Paraná, on corruption charges[.\[19\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-19) Lula and Janja married on 18 May 2022[.\[20\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-:2-20)

# Education and work

Lula had little formal education. He did not learn to read until he was ten years old[.\[21\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-Fourth-time-21) He quit school after the second grade to work. His first job at age eight was as a street vendor.[\[22\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-:3-22) When he was 12, he also worked as a shoeshiner. In 1960, when he was 14, he got his first formal job, in a warehouse[.\[23\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-Biography-23)

In 1961, he started working as an apprentice of a press operator in a metallurgical company that produced screws, while studying in a vocational course. There, Lula had his first contact with strike movements.[\[24\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-24) After the movement failed in its negotiations, Lula left the company for another metallurgical company. From 1966 to 1980 he worked at [Villares Metals S. A,](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Villares_Metals_S. A&action=edit&redlink=1) a new metalworking firm.[\[15\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-cunning-15)

There, in 1974, he lost his left pinky finger in a machinery accident, while working as a press operator in the factory[.\[21\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-Fourth-time-21) After the accident, he had to run to several hospitals before he received medical attention. This experience increased his interest in participating in the Workers' Union. Around that time, he became involved in union activities and held several union posts.[\[23\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-Biography-23)[\[25\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-LULAC1-25)

# Union career

Inspired by his brother Frei Chico, a member of the [Brazilian Communist Party,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Communist_Party) Lula joined the [labour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labour_movement) [movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labour_movement) when he worked at Villares Metals, rising through the ranks[.\[26\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-26) He was elected in 1975, and re-

## ![](_page_2_Picture_12.jpeg)

Lula lost his left pinky in a workrelated accident when he was a metalworker in 1974

elected in 1978, as president of the Steel Workers' Union of [São Bernardo do Campo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Bernardo_do_Campo) and [Diadema.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diadema,_Brazil) Both cities are located in the [ABCD Region,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABCD_Region) home to most of Brazil's automobile manufacturing facilities, including [Ford,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ford_do_Brasil) [Volkswagen,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volkswagen_do_Brasil) [Toyota,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toyota) and [Mercedes-Benz.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercedes-Benz)

In the late 1970s, when Brazil was under military rule, Lula helped organize union activities, including major [strikes.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1978-1980_ABC_Paulista_strikes) [Labour courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labour_court) found the strikes illegal, and in 1980 Lula was jailed for a month. Due to this, and like other people imprisoned for political activities under the military government, Lula was awarded a lifetime pension after the fall of the military regime[.\[13\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-story-13)

# Political career

On 10 February 1980, a group of academics and union leaders, including Lula, founded the [Partido dos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Workers%27_Party_(Brazil)) [Trabalhadores](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Workers%27_Party_(Brazil)) (PT) or Workers' Party, a left-wing party with [progressive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressivism) ideas.[\[27\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-27)[\[28\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-28)[\[29\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-29)[\[30\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-30) In 1983, he helped found the [Central Única dos Trabalhadores](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_%C3%9Anica_dos_Trabalhadores) (CUT) trade union association.[\[31\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-31)

### Elections

Lula first ran for office in 1982 for the state government of [São Paulo,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Paulo_(state)) but lost with 11% of the vote. Cuban president [Fidel Castro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fidel_Castro) urged him to continue on as a politician, during a trip by Lula to Cuba.[\[32\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-32)[\[33\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-33) In the 1986 election, Lula won a seat in the [National Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Congress_of_Brazil) with the most votes nationwide.[\[34\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-34)

In 1989, Lula ran for president as the PT candidate. Lula advocated immediate land reform and that Brazil [default](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Default_(finance)) on its [external debt.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/External_debt) A minor candidate, [Fernando Collor de Mello,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_Collor_de_Mello) quickly amassed support with a more businessfriendly agenda and by taking emphatic anti-corruption positions. He beat Lula in the second round of the [1989](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989_Brazilian_presidential_election) [elections.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989_Brazilian_presidential_election) Lula decided not to run for re-election as a Congressman in 1990[.\[35\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-35)

Lula ran again for president, and lost again, in the next two Brazilian elections. Former [PSDB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PSDB) Minister of Finance [Fernando Henrique Cardoso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_Henrique_Cardoso) defeated Lula who received only 27% of the vote in the presidential elections in [1994,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_Brazilian_general_election) and again, by an even wider margin, as Lula garnered only 32% of the vote in [1998.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_Brazilian_general_election)

## ![](_page_3_Picture_10.jpeg)

Lula speaking at the plenary of the [Chamber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chamber_of_Deputies_of_Brazil) [of Deputies,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chamber_of_Deputies_of_Brazil) 1989

An article in [The Washington Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Washington_Post) said that before 2002 Lula had been a "strident [union organizer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_organizer) known for his bushy beard and [Che Guevara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Che_Guevara) Tshirts".[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-WPost-36) In the 2002 campaign, Lula abandoned both his informal clothing style and his platform plank that Brazil should not pay its foreign debt unless it links the payment to a prior thorough audit. This last point had worried economists, businessmen, and banks, who feared that even a partial Brazilian default would have a massive ripple effect through the world economy. Lula in the [2002 election,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_Brazilian_general_election) defeated PSDB candidate [José](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Serra) [Serra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Serra) in a runoff, to become the country's first [leftist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leftist) president following the fall of the [military dictatorship in Brazil.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_dictatorship_in_Brazil) In the [2006 election,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Brazilian_general_election) Lula won a [run-o](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-round_system)ff over the PSDB's [Geraldo Alckmin.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geraldo_Alckmin)[\[37\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-2006-election-37)

In September 2018, Brazil's top electoral court banned Lula from running for president in [2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Brazilian_general_election) due to his corruption conviction, in accordance with Brazil's [Lei da Ficha Limpa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lei_da_Ficha_Limpa) law.[\[38\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-38) Instead, [Fernando Haddad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_Haddad) ran for president on the Workers Party ticket, and was defeated by [Jair Bolsonaro.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jair_Bolsonaro)[\[39\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-39)

# First presidency (2003–2011)

Lula, described as left-wing, served two terms as president from 2003 through 2010.[\[40\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-40)[\[41\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-41) During his farewell speech he said he felt a burden to prove that he could handle the presidency despite his humble beginnings. "If I failed, it would be the workers' class which would be failing; it would be this country's poor who would be proving they did not have what it takes to rule"[.\[42\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-42)[\[43\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-galeria-43)

## Political orientation

Very few of the reforms that Lula proposed were actually implemented during Lula's terms of office. Some wings of the Worker's Party disagreed with the increasing moderation in focus since the late eighties, and left the party to form other parties, such as during Lula's presidency, the [Socialism and Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism_and_Liberty_Party) [Party.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism_and_Liberty_Party) Alliances with old, traditional oligarch politicians, like former presidents [José Sarney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Sarney) and Fernando Collor, have been a cause of disappointment for some[.\[44\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-44)

### Education

A number of educational initiatives were launched during Lula's first presidency. A free school meals program was extended to 37 million pupils while a programme was launched which aimed to provide "whole or partial remission of student fees for low-income students"[.\[13\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-story-13) In 2006, primary education was extended from 8 to 9 years. A Fund for the Maintenance and Development of Basic Education was set up to improve the quality of education. The PED (an education development plan) conditioned the disbursement of public funds to state schools on the schools' performance.[\[45\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-45)

## ![](_page_4_Picture_11.jpeg)

Lula at the beginning of his first (left) and second (right) terms

Still, in 2006 many Brazilians and commentators felt that Lula had not done enough to improve the quality of public education.[\[46\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-46)[\[47\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-47)[\[48\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-48) And in 2010, while education was compulsory for all children in Brazil aged 7 to 14, in practice that requirement was only loosely enforced; 90% of children in rural areas attended school for less than four years, and only 25% of children living in favelas attended school.[\[49\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-49)

# Social programs

Lula's top social program sought to eradicate hunger. It was financed by an increase in tax revenues, coupled with a decrease in government expenditures on both wages and on benefits paid to public employees, as well as a decrease in government expenditures on infrastructure.[\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-50) The program followed the lead of a project that had already been put into practice in 1995 by the Fernando Henrique Cardoso administration, which was named [Bolsa Escola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_policy_in_Brazil#Bolsa_Escola) (School Stipend)[.\[51\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-51) It expanded that initiative with the new [Fome Zero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fome_Zero) ("Zero Hunger") program, which was part of the [Bolsa Família](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolsa_Fam%C3%ADlia) (Family Allowance) plan.[\[52\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-52)[\[53\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-53)

Five months after Lula took office, however, the budget for Fome Zero was cut down a third from its original amount, and one year later, about \$800 million was budgeted toward the program, but only \$130 million of that was actually disbursed[.\[54\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-54) Lula's program was accused of having become more bark than bite, inasmuch as of May 2005, two years after the effort began, the program had fallen far short of expectations[.\[55\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-55)

## ![](_page_5_Picture_4.jpeg)

Lula speech in [Diadema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diadema,_S%C3%A3o_Paulo) launching [subsidized housing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subsidized_housing) and [Bolsa Família](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolsa_Fam%C3%ADlia) credits, 2005.

During Lula's first term, [child malnutrition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Undernutrition_in_children) decreased by 46%. In May 2010, the UN [World Food](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Food_Programme) [Programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Food_Programme) (WFP) awarded Lula the title of "World Champion in the Fight against Hunger".[\[56\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-56) A number of other social projects were introduced during Lula's first presidency.[\[57\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-57)

Lula launched a housing aid program that was larger in scope to the policies developed until then. More than 15 billion euros were invested in water purification and the urbanization of [favelas,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Favela) and more than 40 billion in housing. The government proposed to relocate the poor populations that occupied the "risk zones", prone to floods or landslides; at the end of the day, however, at least 212 people died and at least 15,000 people were made homeless by the [April 2010 Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/April_2010_Rio_de_Janeiro_floods_and_mudslides) floods and mudslides alone. It proposed to then extend the electricity network, to relocate the streets and to improve the precarious housing. The government undertook to democratize access to real estate credit.[\[58\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-58)

### Economy

Lula's first two terms coincided with a strong [boom in commodities prices.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000s_commodities_boom)[\[59\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto36-59)[\[60\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto37-60)[\[61\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto35-61) This fueled an economic boom in Brazil, which in turn allowed Lula to spend heavily on social programs and pay off a \$15 billion IMF loan a year early.[\[59\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto36-59)[\[60\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto37-60)[\[61\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto35-61)

In the run-up to the 2002 elections, the fear of Lula taking drastic measures, and comparisons of him with [Hugo Chávez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez) of [Venezuela,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuela) increased internal market speculation. This led to a drop in the value of the [Brazilian real,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_real) and a downgrade of Brazil's [credit rating.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Credit_rating)[\[62\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-Downgrade-62) Lula chose [Henrique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henrique_Meirelles) [Meirelles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henrique_Meirelles) of the [Brazilian Social Democracy Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Social_Democracy_Party) (PDSB), a market-oriented economist, as head of the [Brazilian Central Bank.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Central_Bank) As a former CEO of [Bank Boston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank Boston) he was well known to the market[.\[63\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-Meir Bio-63)

Lula and his cabinet followed, to an extent, the lead of the previous government in economics.[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-64) It renewed all agreements with the [International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Monetary_Fund) [Monetary Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Monetary_Fund) (IMF), which were signed by the time Argentina defaulted on its own deals in 2001. His government achieved a satisfactory primary [budget surplus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Budget_surplus) in the first two years, as required by the IMF agreement, exceeding the target for the third year. In late 2005, the government paid off its debt to the IMF in full, two years ahead of schedule[.\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-IMF-65) The Brazilian economy was generally not affected by the 2005 [Mensalão scandal,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mensal%C3%A3o_scandal) which related to vote buying in the Brazilian Congress.[\[66\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-66)

In June 2005, economist and attorney [José Dirceu,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Dirceu) Lula's chief-of-staff since 2003l, resigned after he was caught up in a massive corruption scheme in the legislature, the Mensalão corruption scandal.[\[67\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-67)[\[68\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-68)[\[69\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto27-69)[\[70\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-70) In March 2006, Lula's finance minister [Antonio Palocci,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_Palocci) who had continued the anti-inflation and promarket policies of the previous centrist government, resigned due to his involvement in a corruption and [abuse of power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abuse_of_power) scandal[.\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-71)[\[72\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-72)[\[73\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-73) Lula then appointed [Guido Mantega,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guido_Mantega) a PT economist, as finance minister.

Not long after the start of his second term, in 2007 Lula's government announced the [Growth Acceleration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Growth_Acceleration_Program) [Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Growth_Acceleration_Program) (Programa de Aceleração de Crescimento, PAC), an investment program which sought to solve many of the problems that prevented the Brazilian economy from expanding more rapidly. The measures included investment in the creation and repair of roads and railways, simplification and reduction of taxation, and modernization of the country's energy production to avoid further shortages. The money pledged to be spent on this program was to be around [R\\$](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_real) 500 billion (US \$260 billion) over four years. However, by 2010 many projects remained mired in bureaucracy, and only 11% of the projects outlined in the plan had been completed, while just over half had not even been launched[.\[74\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto26-74)

Prior to taking office, Lula had been a critic of [privatization.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privatization)[\[75\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-75) His administration created [public-private](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public-private_partnership) [partnership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public-private_partnership) concessions for seven federal roadways.[\[76\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-76) After decades with the largest [foreign debt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_debt) among

[emerging economies,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emerging_economies) Brazil became a net creditor for the first time in January 2008[.\[77\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-77) By mid-2008, both [Fitch Ratings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fitch_Ratings) and [Standard & Poor's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_%26_Poor%27s) had elevated the classification of Brazilian debt from speculative to investment grade. Banks made record profits under Lula's government[.\[78\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-78)

[The crash of Wall Street in 2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_crisis_of_2007%E2%80%932008) might have been a tsunami in the US and Europe, Lula declared, but in Brazil it would be no more than a little 'ripple' ("uma marolinha"). The phrase was seized on by the Brazilian press as proof of Lula's reckless economic ignorance and irresponsibility[.\[79\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-79) In 2008, Brazil enjoyed economic good health to fight the global financial crisis with a large [economic stimulus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_stimulus) lasting, at least, until 2014[.\[80\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-80) According to [The Washington Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Washington_Post): "Under Lula, Brazil became the world's eighth-largest economy, [and] more than 20 million people rose out of acute poverty ..."[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-WPost-36)

At the same time, in 2010 The Wall Street Journal noted that: "[Brazil's] [public sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_sector) is bloated and riddled with corruption. Crime is rampant. Its infrastructure is badly in need of repair and expansion. The business environment is restrictive, with a labor code ripped from the pages of [Benito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benito_Mussolini)

## ![](_page_7_Figure_4.jpeg)

Brazil's deforestation rate declined during Lula's time in office, and reversed under Bolsonaro.[\[81\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-Axios_20221031-81)[\[82\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-Terra Brasilis_1988-82)

[Mussolini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benito_Mussolini)'s economic playbook. Brazil also risks patting itself on the back so much that it fails to see the colossal work that remains to be done.["\[74\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto26-74)

### Environment

Initially, Lula's administration pushed for progressive policies that significantly curbed deforestation in the Amazon. Despite this, he did not support legislation that would have required the country to phase out its fossil fuels.

In May 2008, environmental minister [Marina Silva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marina_Silva) resigned, blamed "stagnation" in the government, after she lost disagreements with Lula when she opposed approval of new [hydroelectric dams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydroelectric_dam) in the Amazon and criticized Lula's [biofuels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biofuels) program.[\[83\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-83) Dr. Daniel Nepstad of the [Woods Hole Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woods_Hole_Research_Center) said the growing demand for biofuels may ultimately result in more Amazon deforestation.[\[84\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-84) In particular, environmentalists warn that while biofuels reduce emissions of [greenhouse gases,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenhouse_gas) they may well also trigger a significant expansion of the biofuel crops; that, in turn, may pushe agriculture deeper into forests, destroying habitat and [biodiversity.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity)[\[85\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-85)

The creation of conservation areas and indigenous reserves led to a decrease of around 75% in deforestation starting in 2004[.\[86\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-86) In Lula's first year in office, in 2003-04, 25,000 [square kilometers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Square_kilometer) of Brazilian forest were destroyed, the second-worst devastation since 1977[.\[87\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto32-87) In late 2006, the Instituto Soicioambiental environmental group said that deforestation in Lula's first four years had been worse than in any four-year period since 1988.[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-88)[\[87\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto32-87)[\[89\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-89) By 2009, Brazil's Amazon destruction--though lower--was still about 7,000 square kilometers a year, larger than the US state of [Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware)[.\[90\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto29-90) Critics said, however, that Brazil's lowest rate of deforestation in 2009 was a function of the global financial crisis.[\[91\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto30-91) Paulo Adario of [Greenpeace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenpeace) said that it was a function not of efforts to protect the climate, but of the fact that the "demand for beef, soya and wood ha[d] dramatically fallen".[\[91\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto30-91)

In 2009, Lula gave a speech in which he said that "gringos" should pay Amazon nations to prevent deforestation[.\[90\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto29-90)

In February 2010, Lula's government approved the construction of a controversial hydroelectric mega [Belo Monte Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belo_Monte_Dam) in the middle of the Amazon rain forest in the Brazilian state of [Pará.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Par%C3%A1)[\[91\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto30-91) It was to be the third-largest hydroelectric dam in the world[.\[91\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto30-91) Environmental activists protested the building of the dam[.\[91\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto30-91) It was expected to cause a significant decline in the [water table,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_table) resulting in significant losses of aquatic and terrestrial [fauna,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fauna) and adversely impact [aquatic mammals.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aquatic_mammal)[\[92\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto33-92) Approximately 20,000-40,000 indigenous people were to be resettled with little or no compensation, and 516 square kilometers (199 square miles) of rain forest were to be flooded for the dam's construction.[\[91\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto30-91)[\[92\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto33-92)

### Foreign policy

In 1979, Lula was asked in an interview which historical figures he admired most. He answered: [Gandhi,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gandhi) [Che Guevara,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Che_Guevara) and [Mao Zedong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mao_Zedong)[.\[93\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto28-93) Upon being asked to give additional examples, he added [Hitler,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hitler) [Castro,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fidel_Castro) and [Ayatollah Khomeini,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayatollah_Khomeini) saying: "I admire in a man the fire to want to do something, and then his going out to try to do it."[\[94\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-94)[\[93\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto28-93)

Leading a large agricultural state, Lula generally opposed and criticized farm subsidies, and this position has been seen as one of the reasons for the walkout of developing nations and subsequent collapse of the Cancún [World Trade Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization) talks in 2003 over [G8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G8) agricultural subsidies.[\[95\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-time-95) Brazil played a role in negotiations regarding internal conflicts in Venezuela and [Colombia,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colombia) and made efforts to strengthen [Mercosur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercosur)[.\[96\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-ft-96) During the Lula administration, Brazilian foreign trade increased dramatically, changing from deficits to several surpluses after 2003. In 2004, the surplus was US\$29 billion, due to a substantial increase in global demand for commodities. Brazil also provided UN peace-keeping troops and led a peace-keeping mission in [Haiti.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haiti)[\[97\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-97)

According to [The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist) of 2 March 2006, Lula had a pragmatic foreign policy, seeing himself as a negotiator, not an ideologue, a leader adept at reconciling opposites. As a result, he befriended both Venezuelan president [Hugo Chávez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez) and U. S. President [George W. Bush.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_W._Bush)[\[98\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-98) Former [Finance Minister,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finance_Minister) and current advisor, Delfim Netto, said: "Lula is the ultimate pragmatist".[\[99\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-99)

He travelled to more than 80 countries during his presidency[.\[100\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-al_jaz-100) A goal of Lula's foreign policy was for the country to gain a seat as a permanent member of the United Nations Security Council. In this he was unsuccessful[.\[100\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-al_jaz-100)

#### China

From 2003 to 2010, Lula embraced China as central to reforming what he considered an unjust global order[.\[101\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto11-101) He intertwined the Chinese and Brazilian economies[.\[102\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-102)[\[103\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-103)[\[101\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto11-101) Lula stated Brazil's commitment to the One China principle that is the position held by the [People's Republic of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People%27s_Republic_of_China) and the ruling [Chinese Communist Party,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Communist_Party) saying that the government of the People's Republic of China was the sole legal government representing the whole of China, including [Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan) -- as part of China.[\[104\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-104)

## ![](_page_8_Picture_10.jpeg)

Lula and Cuban leader [Fidel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fidel_Castro) [Castro,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fidel_Castro) 2003

#### Cuba

Lula and Cuban president [Fidel Castro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fidel_Castro) were longtime friends.[\[105\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-105)[\[106\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-106) Under Lula, Brazil provided money and corporate support to Cuba.[\[107\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-107)[\[108\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto3-108) The state-controlled Brazilian oil company [Petrobras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrobras) studied the possibility of drilling for oil off of Cuba, while the [Odebrecht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odebrecht) construction firm headed a revamp of the Cuban port of [Mariel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariel,_Cuba) into the island's main commercial port.[\[108\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto3-108)[\[109\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto2-109) Brazil's state-run [Brazilian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Development_Bank) [Development Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Development_Bank) gave \$300 million to Odebrecht to build new roads, rail lines, wharves, and warehouses at Mariel.[\[108\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto3-108) Brazil also offered Cuba up to \$1 billion in [credit lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_of_credit) to pay for Brazilian goods and services[.\[109\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto2-109)

#### Iraq

In 2003, Lula condemned the U. S.-led [invasion of Iraq,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_invasion_of_Iraq) saying that the United States had no right "to decide unilaterally what is good and what is bad for the world".[\[110\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-110) He said that "the behaviour of the United States in relation to Iraq has weakened the United Nations".[\[111\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-111)

#### Iran

The conviction by an Iranian court of Iranian [Sakineh Mohammadi Ashtiani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sakineh_Mohammadi_Ashtiani) for the crime of adultery, with a sentence in 2006 of execution by stoning, led to calls for Lula to intercede on her behalf. In July 2010 Lula said "I need to respect the laws of a [foreign] country. If my friendship with the president of Iran and the respect that I have for him is worth something, if this woman has become a nuisance, we will receive her in Brazil". The Iranian government declined his offer[.\[112\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-washingtonpost1-112)[\[113\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-113) [Mina Ahadi,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mina_Ahadi) an Iranian Communist politician, welcomed Lula's offer, but reiterated a call for an end to stoning altogether and requested a cessation of recognition and support for the Iranian government[.\[114\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-114)[\[115\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-115)[\[116\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-116)[\[117\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-117) [Jackson Diehl,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jackson_Diehl) deputy [editorial page](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Editorial_page) editor of [The Washington Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Washington_Post), called Lula the "best friend of tyrants in the democratic world," and criticised his actions.[\[112\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-washingtonpost1-112) [Shirin Ebadi,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shirin_Ebadi) Iranian human rights activist and Nobel Peace Prize laureate called Lula's comments a "powerful message to the Islamic Republic".[\[118\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-118)

In 2009, Lula warmly hosted Iranian president [Ahmadinejad,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmadinejad) who made a controversial visit to Iran.[\[119\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-119) [\[120\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-120) Some demonstrators expressed displeasure over Ahmadinejad's positions on human rights and his denial of [the Holocaust.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust)[\[121\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-121)

In May 2010, Lula and Turkey's prime minister [Recep Tayyip Erdo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recep_Tayyip_Erdo%C4%9Fan)ğan negotiated a preliminary fuel swap agreement with the Iranian government on [uranium enrichment,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uranium_enrichment) that ultimately failed.[\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto19-122) The preliminary agreement that they presented to the United Nations was at odds with what the [International Atomic Energy Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Atomic_Energy_Agency) and other countries viewed as

necessary actions to stop Iran from obtaining [weapons grade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weapons_grade) materials[.\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto19-122) And within hours of signing the agreement, Iran did an about-face and announced that it would continue to enrich some uranium.[\[123\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto20-123) U. S. Secretary of State [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton) said Brazil was being "used" by Tehran[.\[123\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto20-123) The [UN Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Security_Council) ultimately rejected it when permanent member country representatives argued that "the swap proposal negotiated by Brazil and Turkey would leave Iran with enough material to make a nuclear weapon," and that "Iran intends to continue a new program of enriching uranium to a higher level.["\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto19-122)[\[100\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-al_jaz-100)[\[124\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-124) [Pulitzer Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulitzer_Prize) winning journalist [Thomas Friedman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Friedman) wrote: "Is there anything uglier than watching democrats sell out other democrats to a Holocaust-denying, vote-stealing Iranian thug just to tweak the U. S. and show that they, too, can play at the big power table?["\[125\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto21-125) [Moisés Naím,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mois%C3%A9s_Na%C3%ADm) editor in chief of [Foreign Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Policy) magazine and former Minister of Trade in Venezuela, said "Lula is a political giant, but morally he has been a deep disappointment."[\[125\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto21-125) In 2010, in addition, Brazilians largely disagreed with Lula as to how to handle Iran and Iran's nuclear weapons program[.\[126\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto10-126) While Lula opposed additional international economic sanctions against Iran, of the 85% of Brazilians who opposed Iran acquiring nuclear weapons, two-thirds approved of tighter international sanctions on Iran to try to prevent it from developing nuclear weapons.[\[126\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto10-126)

#### Libya

Brazil, as a non-permanent member of the [UN Security Council,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Security_Council) abstained from the vote authorising "all necessary measures" against Libya's [Muammar Gadda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muammar_Gaddafi)fi. [\[127\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto38-127) It opposed the [bombing in Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_military_intervention_in_Libya) to implement [United Nations Security Council Resolution 1973.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_1973)[\[127\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto38-127) Lula said: "These invasions only happen because the United Nations is weak."[\[127\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto38-127)

#### Venezuela

Lula was close with Venezuela's president [Hugo Chávez,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez) a close ally of Communist Cuba and an antagonist of the United States[.\[128\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto8-128)[\[129\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto9-129) In November 2007, Lula defended Chávez as the democratic choice of his people[.\[129\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto9-129) He said: "There is no risk with Chávez.["\[129\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto9-129) Expressing his admiration for Chávez, he said "Only thanks to Chávez's leadership, the people [of Venezuela] have had extraordinary achievements," and that in 2008 that Chávez was "the best president the country has had in 100 years."[\[130\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-130) Brazilians had a different view than Lula in 2010, however, as only 13% had at least some confidence in Chávez, while seven-in-ten had little or no confidence in him.[\[126\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto10-126)

# Freedom of the press

After Lula was infuriated by a May 9, 2004, New York Times article that claimed he had a drinking problem, Brazil ordered the New York Times reporter, [Larry Rohter,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larry_Rohter) to leave the country and revoked his visa because he had written a story "offensive to the honour of the president."[\[131\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-131)[\[132\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto23-132)

Lula said: "Certainly its author ... must be more worried than I am ... it deserves action."[\[132\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto23-132) Brazil's presidential palace threatened to take legal action against the New York Times, which stood by the story and said that the expulsion raised serious questions about [freedom of expression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_expression) and [freedom of the press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_the_press) in Brazil.[\[132\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto23-132) No journalist had been expelled from Brazil since its military dictatorship ended in the mid-1980s[.\[132\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto23-132) Brazilian opposition senator [Tasso Jereissati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tasso_Jereissati) said: "This is ridiculous. It's more like the immature act of a dictator of a third-rate republic..."[\[132\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto23-132) Brazil's second largest union, [Força Sindical,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/For%C3%A7a_Sindical) issued a statement expressing concern that: "it is a reaction typical of authoritarian governments that don't like contrary voices."[\[132\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto23-132) Despite criticism, on May 13, 2004, Lula said "he would not consider revoking the action."[\[132\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto23-132) The government subsequently changed its position, and allowed the reporter to remain[.\[133\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto22-133)

Three months later Lula introduced legislation to create a Brazil National Journalists' Council that would have the power to "orient, discipline and monitor" journalists and their work[.\[133\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto22-133)[\[134\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto24-134) Critics called the draft law the worst affront to press freedom since censorship under the military dictatorship[.\[133\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto22-133)[\[134\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto24-134) The government also proposed the establishment of a National Cinema and Audiovisual Agency that would have the power to conduct prior reviews of programming and to veto certain programs if they believed that they did not to meet standards of "editorial responsibility.["\[133\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto22-133)

# Corruption scandals and controversy

Lula's administration was plagued by numerous corruption scandals, notably the [Mensalão \\$50 million](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mensal%C3%A3o_scandal) [vote-buying scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mensal%C3%A3o_scandal) and [Escândalo dos sanguessugas](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Esc%C3%A2ndalo_dos_sanguessugas&action=edit&redlink=1) during Lula's first term.[\[135\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-135)[\[136\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-136)[\[137\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-137)

### Mensalão vote-buying scandal

In the 2005 Mensalão \$50 million vote-buying scandal, Brazilian attorney general Álvaro Augusto Ribeiro Costa presented charges against 40 politicians and officials involved in the Mensalão affair, including several charges against Lula himself. Lula said that he knew nothing about the scandals[.\[138\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-naosabia-138)

Among those convicted were [Jose Dirceu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jose_Dirceu) (who was Lula's chief of staff and right-hand man from 2003 to 2005; he was sentenced to over 10 years in jail), and both the former head of Lula's Workers Party, [Jose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jose_Genoino) [Genoino,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jose_Genoino) and its treasurer, [Delubio Soares.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delubio_Soares)[\[69\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto27-69)[\[139\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-139) Dirceu and officials [Luiz Gushiken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_Gushiken) and [Humberto Costa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humberto_Costa) said that Lula was not involved. [Roberto Je](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roberto_Jefferson)fferson said that if Lula didn't "commit a crime by action, he committed it by omission."[\[140\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-140) But one of Lula's own party members, [Arlindo Chinaglia,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arlindo_Chinaglia) alleged that Lula had been warned about the matter, and businessman [Marcos Valério,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcos_Val%C3%A9rio) who was sentenced to more than 40

years in prison for his involvement in the scandal, alleged in testimony after he was convicted that Lula had authorized loans for the scheme and used some of the money to pay for his personal expenses[.\[141\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-141)[\[142\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-142)

### Politicking

His administration was heavily criticized for relying on local, right-of-centre political barons, like [José](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Sarney) [Sarney,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Sarney) [Jader Barbalho,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jader_Barbalho) [Renan Calheiros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renan_Calheiros) and Fernando Collor to ensure a majority in [Congress.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_Brazil) Another frequent reproach was his ambiguous treatment of the left wing of the PT. Analysts felt that he would occasionally give in to left-wing calls for tighter government control on media and increased state intervention: in 2004, he pushed for the creation of a "Federal Council of Journalists" (CFJ) and a "National Cinema Agency" (Ancinav), the latter designed to overhaul funding for [electronic communications.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronic_media) Both proposals ultimately failed amid concerns over the effect of state control on free speech[.\[143\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-143)[\[144\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-144)

## Great Recession caused by white people with blue eyes

Before a [G-20 summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G-20_summit) in London in March 2009, Lula caused an uproar by declaring that the [economic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession) [crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession) was caused by "the irrational behavior of white people with blue eyes, who before seemed to know everything, and now have shown they don't know anything".[\[145\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-145) He added: "I don't know any black or indigenous bankers."[\[146\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto5-146) He repeated the accusation the following month[.\[146\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-auto5-146)

### Terrorist Cesare Battisti

When Italian far-left terrorist [Cesare Battisti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cesare_Battisti_(1954)) of the [Armed Proletarians for Communism,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armed_Proletarians_for_Communism) wanted for four murders, was arrested in Rio de Janeiro in March 2007 by Brazilian and French police officers, Brazilian Minister of Justice [Tarso Genro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarso_Genro) granted him status as a political refugee. It was a controversial decision, which divided Italy and the Brazilian and international press. In February 2009, the [European Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Parliament) adopted a resolution in support of Italy, and held a minute's silence in memory of Battisti's victims. In November 2009, the Brazilian Supreme Court declared the grant of refugee status illegal and allowed Battisi's extradition, but also stated that the Brazilian constitution gave the president the personal power to deny the extradition if he chose to, effectively putting the final decision in the hands of Lula.[\[147\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-147)

Lula barred Battisti's extradition.[\[148\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-148) On 31 December 2010, Lula's last day in office, his decision not to allow Battisti's extradition was officially announced. Battisti was released on 9 June 2011 from prison, after the Brazilian Constitutional Court denied Italy's request to extradite him. Italy planned to appeal to the [International Court of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Court_of_Justice) in the Hague, saying Brazil had breached an extradition treaty.[\[149\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva#cite_note-149) Lula's successor,
